Renormalization of 𝑼 ⁡ ( 𝟏 ) \bm{U(1)} Gauge Boson Kinetic Mixing
Felix Forner 1, Felix Tellander 2,3

Abstract
. Quantum field theories containing fields with the same quantum numbers allow for mixed kinetic terms in the Lagrangian, leading to off-diagonal elements in the tree-level two-point function. After removing the mixing by a field rotation, the off-diagonal UV divergences cannot be subtracted by a counterterm, still one can show that the theory is renormalizable. We study kinetic mixing of U ⁡ ( 1 ) U(1) gauge bosons in an extension of QED with a massive "dark" photon at one-loop order. In general covariant R ξ R_{\xi} -gauge, the gauge-fixing function naively obstructs the removal of tree-level mixing but we show that these off-diagonal gauge-dependent contributions cancel. We compare two renormalization schemes: one with and one without kinetic mixing, and relate them via a scale-dependent field transformation, showing that the schemes are equivalent.



TUM-HEP-1580/25

1.
Introduction

Renormalizability is key for a quantum field theory to be consistent. In many standard cases, the UV divergences can be subtracted through counterterms at the level of each individual amputated Green’s function. However, this is not always possible if the fields mix at loop level. Known examples with this feature are theories with several fields
\Phi_{i}
with the same quantum numbers. In these cases, one may allow these fields to mix through non-diagonal kinetic terms in the Lagrangian,

\mathcal{L}\supset\sum\limits_{i<j}\frac{\kappa_{ij}}{2}\partial_{\mu}\Phi_{i}\partial^{\mu}\Phi_{j}.

These terms may be rotated away at tree-level but mixing will reappear at loop-level. Divergences from the off-diagonal two-point Green’s functions
G_{ij}
must be absorbed by a sort of non-diagonal renormalization. Thus, even if one rotates away
\kappa_{ij}
initially, one might think that it must be reintroduced at tree-level to provide a counterterm that can cancel this divergence. For scalar fields, it was decisively shown to one-loop order in
[
1
]
that
\kappa_{ij}
can be rotated away at tree-level and that the theory can be consistently renormalized without reintroducing
\kappa_{ij}
. The resolution lies in the basic fact that it is only physical quantities that are in need of renormalization, and the two-point functions themselves are not physical; only their poles and S-matrix elements.

As is well-known, in theories without kinetic mixing, the reduction formula of Lehmann, Symanzik, and Zimmerman (LSZ)
[
2
]
relates
S
-matrix elements directly to amputated, renormalized correlation functions.
It is important to note that these two objects differ in a theory with kinetic mixing, since one has to modify the LSZ theorem to account for mixing on the external legs
[
3
]
when computing
S
-matrix elements. Then, the off-diagonal pieces of
G_{ij}
contribute to the
S
-matrix elements, and thus, they do enter the computation of physical quantities. However, it is possible to define a consistent scheme, without reintroducing
\kappa_{ij}
, where these divergences are absorbed in counterterms of the couplings.

In this work, we address this matter in the context of kinetic mixing of two
U(1)
gauge bosons. We work in general covariant
R_{\xi}
-gauge and demonstrate to one-loop order that the theory can be renormalized consistently, both in a basis with and without kinetic mixing, and that the results in the two bases match.

On the level of the Lagrangian, removing the off-diagonal kinetic terms produces non-diagonal gauge fixing functions, which still lead to a non-diagonal tree-level propagator.
We show, however, that these gauge-dependent off-diagonal contributions cancel and that the field redefinition is consistent. We work with standard QED with an additional massive,
dark photon
, which does not couple directly to the electron but interacts only through a mixed kinetic term with the photon. This model setup is inspired by popular Standard Model extensions, e.g.
[
4
,
5
,
6
,
7
,
8
,
9
,
10
,
11
]
, where many of the phenomenological consequences of a kinetically mixed massive dark photon are explored, in particular the possibility of dark photon dark matter. To give a non-vanishing gauge-invariant mass to the dark photon, we employ the Stückelberg mechanism
[
12
,
13
]
.

This paper is organized as follows. First, in
section

2
we introduce the QED extension with kinetic mixing, renormalize it at one-loop order and derive the renormalization group equations (RGEs). In
section

3
we do the same in a basis without kinetic mixing, where we only renormalize the physical parameters. We relate the two schemes in
section

4
and conclude in
section

5
.

2.
QED Extension with U(1) Gauge Boson Kinetic Mixing

We consider QED with kinetic mixing between the photon and an additional, massive
U(1)
gauge boson, which we will refer to as a dark photon. The theory is defined by the (bare) Lagrangian

(1)

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}=&-\frac{1}{4}(F_{1\,b}^{\mu\nu})^{2}+i\overline{\psi}_{b}\gamma_{\mu}(\partial^{\mu}+ie_{b}A_{1\,b}^{\mu})\psi_{b}-m_{b}\overline{\psi}_{b}\psi_{b}-\frac{1}{2\xi_{1\,b}}(\partial_{\mu}A_{1\,b}^{\mu})^{2}-\frac{\kappa_{b}}{2}F_{1\,b}^{\mu\nu}F_{2\,b,\mu\nu}\\
&-\frac{1}{4}(F_{2\,b}^{\mu\nu})^{2}+\frac{M_{b}^{2}}{2}\left(A_{2\,b}^{\mu}-\frac{1}{M_{b}}\partial^{\mu}\phi_{b}\right)^{2}-\frac{1}{2\xi_{2\,b}}(\partial_{\mu}A_{2\,b}^{\mu}+\xi_{2\,b}M_{b}\phi_{b})^{2},\end{split}

where
A_{1\,b}
describes the photon,
A_{2\,b}
the dark photon with mass
M_{b}
;
\psi_{b}
the electron with mass
m_{b}
, and we define
F_{i\,b}^{\mu\nu}=\partial^{\mu}A_{i\,b}^{\nu}-\partial^{\nu}A_{i\,b}^{\mu},\ i=1,2,

as usual. We denote the symmetry group by
U(1)_{\text{QED}}\times U(1)_{\text{dark}}
.
The second line in the above Lagrangian is the Stückelberg Lagrangian. The Stückelberg mechanism
[
12
,
13
]
allows us to give a mass to the dark photon in a gauge invariant way, by introducing the Stückelberg field
\phi_{b}
. For the Lagrangian (
1
) to be invariant under the gauge transformations

(2)

\displaystyle A_{1\,b}^{\mu}(x)\xrightarrow{U(1)_{\text{QED}}}A_{1\,b}^{\mu}(x)-\frac{1}{e_{b}}\partial^{\mu}\alpha_{1}(x)\quad\mathrm{and}\quad A_{2\,b}^{\mu}(x)\xrightarrow{U(1)_{\text{dark}}}A_{2\,b}^{\mu}(x)-\partial^{\mu}\alpha_{2}(x),

we require the Stückelberg field to transform trivially under
U(1)_{\text{QED}}
and as

(3)

\displaystyle\phi_{b}(x)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{U(1)_{\text{dark}}}\phi_{b}(x)-M_{b}\alpha_{2}(x)

under
U(1)_{\text{dark}}
, such that the mass term of the dark photon is invariant. For the gauge fixing term of
A_{2\,b}
to be invariant as well, we impose for
\alpha_{2}(x)
the Klein-Gordon equation

(4)

\displaystyle(\partial^{2}-\xi_{2\,b}M_{b}^{2})\alpha_{2}(x)=0.

To see more clearly that the dark photon field
A_{2\,b}
acquires a mass in this model, note that, up to a total derivative, the Stückelberg Lagrangian may be rewritten as

(5)

\mathcal{L}_{\text{St{\"{u}}ckelberg}}=-\frac{1}{4}(F_{2\,b}^{\mu\nu})^{2}-\frac{1}{2\xi_{2\,b}}(\partial_{\mu}A_{2\,b}^{\mu})^{2}+\frac{M_{b}^{2}}{2}(A_{2\,b}^{\mu})^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{\mu}\phi_{b})^{2}-\xi_{2\,b}\frac{M_{b}^{2}}{2}\phi_{b}^{2}.

For a more detailed review of the Stückelberg mechanism, see, for example,
[
14
]
.

In our Lagrangian in
eq.

1
, there is no direct fermion-dark-photon coupling. The only coupling of the dark photon is through the kinetic mixing to the photon, parametrised by
\kappa_{b}
. We will now renormalize this theory at one-loop and derive the RGEs. First, we compute the gauge boson propagator and full two-point function, which will be matrix-valued due to the mixing.

2.1.
Two-Point Function and Masses

The tree-level propagator
P
is found from a fundamental solution to the equations of motion. The equations of motion from the Lagrangian (
1
) are

(6)

\begin{pmatrix}\partial^{2}g_{\mu\nu}-\left(1-\frac{1}{\xi_{1\,b}}\right)\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}&\kappa_{b}\left(\partial^{2}g_{\mu\nu}-\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\right)\\
\kappa_{b}\left(\partial^{2}g_{\mu\nu}-\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\right)&(\partial^{2}+M_{b}^{2})g_{\mu\nu}-\left(1-\frac{1}{\xi_{2\,b}}\right)\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}A_{1\,b}^{\mu}\\
A_{2\,b}^{\mu}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}J^{QED}_{b,\nu}\\
0\end{pmatrix},

where
J^{QED}_{b,\nu}=\overline{\psi}_{b}(x)\gamma_{\nu}\psi_{b}(x)
is the standard QED current. By Fourier transforming this matrix and inverting it as a block-matrix, we find the matrix-valued propagator

(7)

\displaystyle iP=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{-i}{p^{2}}\left(A(p^{2})\left(g_{\mu\nu}-\frac{p_{\mu}p_{\nu}}{p^{2}}\right)+\xi_{1\,b}\frac{p_{\mu}p_{\nu}}{p^{2}}\right)&\frac{i\kappa_{b}}{(1-\kappa_{b}^{2})p^{2}-M_{b}^{2}}\left(g^{\mu\nu}-\frac{p^{\mu}p^{\nu}}{p^{2}}\right)\\
\frac{i\kappa_{b}}{(1-\kappa_{b}^{2})p^{2}-M_{b}^{2}}\left(g^{\mu\nu}-\frac{p^{\mu}p^{\nu}}{p^{2}}\right)&\frac{-i}{\left(1-\kappa_{b}^{2}\right)p^{2}-M_{b}^{2}}\left(g^{\mu\nu}-\frac{p^{\mu}p^{\nu}\left(1-\xi_{2\,b}\left(1-\kappa_{b}^{2}\right)\right)}{\left(p^{2}-\xi_{2\,b}M_{b}^{2}\right)}\right)\end{pmatrix},

where we have defined

(8)

\displaystyle A(p^{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\left(1-\kappa_{b}^{2}\right)}\frac{p^{2}-M_{b}^{2}}{p^{2}-M_{b}^{2}/\left(1-\kappa_{b}^{2}\right)}.

For two fields
A_{i}^{\mu},\ A_{j}^{\nu}
, the full two-point function
G_{ij}^{{\mu\nu}}
is defined as

(9)

\bra{\Omega}T\left\{A_{i}^{\mu}(x)A_{j}^{\nu}(y)\right\}\ket{\Omega}=\int\frac{d^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}e^{ip(x-y)}iG_{ij}^{\mu\nu}(p^{2}),

and we denote its tree-level contribution by
iP_{ij}^{\mu\nu}
. Furthermore, let
i\Pi_{ij}^{\mu\nu}(p^{2})
be the all-order one-particle irreducible (1PI) contribution. Then, suppressing the Lorentz indices
{\mu\nu}
, the two-point function
G_{ij}
can be written as

\displaystyle iG_{ij}

\displaystyle=\hbox to53.41pt{\vbox to15.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\enskip\lower-7.60284pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}+\hbox to138.77pt{\vbox to21.74pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\enskip\lower-10.86978pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}+\cdots

\displaystyle=iP+iP(i\Pi iP)+iP(i\Pi iP)^{2}+\cdots

(10)

\displaystyle=iP(1+\Pi P)^{-1}.

Due to gauge invariance,
\Pi_{ij}^{\mu\nu}(p^{2})
is transversal, i.e.,
p_{\mu}\Pi_{ij}^{\mu\nu}(p^{2})=~0
. Thus, the Lorentz structure can be factored out, and we may write

(11)

\displaystyle\Pi_{ij}^{\mu\nu}(p^{2})=-(p^{2}g^{\mu\nu}-p^{\mu}p^{\nu})\Pi_{ij}(p^{2}).

In the next section, we compute the 1-loop UV divergences and renormalize our theory.

2.2.
Renormalization

To renormalize the theory, we introduce the rescalings

\displaystyle\psi_{b}=\sqrt{Z_{2}}\psi,\qquad

\displaystyle A_{1\,b}^{\mu}=\sqrt{Z_{3}}A_{1}^{\mu},\qquad

\displaystyle A_{2\,b}^{\mu}=\sqrt{Z_{4}}A_{2}^{\mu},\qquad

\displaystyle m_{b}=Z_{m}m,\qquad

\displaystyle e_{b}=\mu^{\epsilon}Z_{e}e,

\displaystyle M_{b}=\sqrt{Z_{M}}M,\qquad

\displaystyle\kappa_{b}=Z_{\kappa}\kappa,\qquad

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\xi_{1\,b}}=Z_{\xi_{1}}\frac{1}{\xi_{1}},\qquad

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\xi_{2\,b}}=Z_{\xi_{2}}\frac{1}{\xi_{2}},\qquad

\displaystyle\phi_{b}=\sqrt{Z_{\phi}}\phi.

The renormalization factors are decomposed into counterterms as
Z_{i}=1+\delta_{i}
, and we define the two combined renormalization factors

\displaystyle Z_{1}

\displaystyle:=Z_{e}Z_{2}\sqrt{Z_{3}}\qquad

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\qquad\delta_{1}

\displaystyle:=\delta_{e}+\delta_{2}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{3},

\displaystyle Z_{\mathrm{mix}}

\displaystyle:=Z_{\kappa}\sqrt{Z_{3}}\sqrt{Z_{2}}\qquad

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\qquad\delta_{\mathrm{mix}}

\displaystyle:=\delta_{\kappa}+\frac{1}{2}(\delta_{3}+\delta_{4}).

The original Lagrangian (
1
) can now be written as a "renormalized" Lagrangian plus the counterterm Lagrangian

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\delta}=

\displaystyle-\frac{\delta_{3}}{4}(F_{1}^{\mu\nu})^{2}+i\delta_{2}\gamma^{\mu}\overline{\psi}\partial^{\mu}\psi-e\delta_{1}\gamma_{\mu}\overline{\psi}A_{1}^{\mu}\psi-m(\delta_{m}+\delta_{2})\overline{\psi}\psi

(12)

\displaystyle-\frac{\delta_{3}+\delta_{\xi_{1}}}{2\xi_{1}}(\partial_{\mu}A_{1}^{\mu})^{2}-\kappa\frac{\delta_{\mathrm{mix}}}{2}F_{1}^{\mu\nu}F_{2\,\mu\nu}-\frac{\delta_{4}}{4}(F_{2}^{\mu\nu})^{2}-\frac{\delta_{4}+\delta_{\xi_{2}}}{2\xi_{2}}(\partial_{\mu}A_{2}^{\mu})^{2}

\displaystyle+(\delta_{M}+\delta_{4})\frac{M^{2}}{2}(A_{2}^{\mu})^{2}+\frac{\delta_{\phi}}{2}(\partial_{\mu}\phi)^{2}-\xi_{2}\frac{M^{2}}{2}(\delta_{\xi_{2}}+\delta_{M}+\delta_{\phi})\phi^{2}.

The Feynman rules for the counterterm Lagrangian are:

(13)

Throughout this paper, for any quantity
f
, we write
f^{[l]}
for the
l
-loop contribution to
f
. There are no amputated loop-diagrams with external dark photons or Stückelberg fields, so there are only three one-loop diagrams we need to compute; the photon and electron self-energy, and the vertex correction, respectively:

\displaystyle i\Pi_{11}^{[1]\,\mu\nu}(p^{2})

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

The latter two of these objects differ from the ones in standard QED by the fact that we have to use the modified propagator
iP_{11}
for the photon, defined through
eq.

7
. However, since we only extract the UV poles, the extra factor
A
simplifies to

(14)

\displaystyle A(p^{2})\xrightarrow{p\to\infty}\frac{1}{1-\kappa^{2}}.

The tensor structures might be simplified, for example, using
FeynCalc

[
15
,
16
]
. After a straightforward computation, we find the UV poles

\displaystyle i\Pi_{11}^{[1]\,\mu\nu}(p^{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{-e^{2}}{12\pi^{2}}(p^{2}g^{\mu\nu}-p^{\mu}p^{\nu})\frac{1}{\epsilon}-i\delta_{3}(p^{2}g^{\mu\nu}-p^{\mu}p^{\nu})-i\frac{\delta_{3}+\delta_{\xi_{1}}}{\xi_{1}}p^{\mu}p^{\nu},

(15)

\displaystyle i\Sigma^{[1]}(\not\!{p})

\displaystyle=\frac{ie^{2}}{16\pi^{2}\epsilon}\left(\xi_{1}(\not\!{p}-m)-3m\frac{1}{1-\kappa^{2}}\right)+i(\not\!{p}\delta_{2}-m(\delta_{m}+\delta_{2})),

\displaystyle i\Gamma_{1}^{[1]\,\mu}(p^{2})

\displaystyle=-\frac{ie^{3}}{16\pi^{2}\epsilon}\xi_{1}\gamma^{\mu}-ie\delta_{1}\gamma^{\mu}.

From here, we find the minimal subtraction (MS) counterterms at one-loop order,

(16)

\displaystyle\delta^{[1]}_{3}

\displaystyle=-\delta^{[1]}_{\xi_{1}}=-\frac{e^{2}}{12\pi^{2}}\frac{1}{\epsilon},\qquad

\displaystyle\delta^{[1]}_{2}=-\frac{e^{2}}{16\pi^{2}\epsilon}\xi_{1},

\displaystyle\delta^{[1]}_{m}

\displaystyle=-\frac{e^{2}}{16\pi^{2}\epsilon}\frac{3}{1-\kappa^{2}},\qquad

\displaystyle\delta^{[1]}_{1}=-\frac{e^{2}}{16\pi^{2}\epsilon}\xi_{1}.

Since there are no one-loop diagrams for the dark-photon self-energy or the mixed photon-dark-photon two-point function, we simply have that the corresponding counterterms in
eq.

13
vanish:

(17)

\displaystyle\delta^{[1]}_{4}=\delta^{[1]}_{M}=\delta^{[1]}_{\xi_{2}}=0,\qquad\delta^{[1]}_{\text{mix}}=0\quad\implies\quad\delta^{[1]}_{\kappa}=-\frac{1}{2}\delta^{[1]}_{3}.

2.3.
Renormalization Group

Here we derive the RGEs to one-loop order. As usual, we define the beta function for a generic coupling
g
as
\beta(g):=\mu\derivative{\mu}g|_{\epsilon=0}
and the anomalous dimension of a generic parameter
\Lambda
as
\gamma_{\Lambda}:=\frac{\mu}{\Lambda}\derivative{\mu}\Lambda|_{\epsilon=0}
. Using that the bare parameters are independent of
\mu
, we get

(18)

\displaystyle 0=\mu\derivative{\mu}e_{b}=\mu\mu^{\epsilon}eZ_{e}\left(\epsilon+\frac{\mu}{e}\derivative{e}{\mu}+\frac{\mu}{Z_{e}}\derivative{Z_e}{\mu}\right),

and for the
\beta
-function

(19)

\displaystyle\beta^{[1]}(e)=\frac{e^{3}}{12\pi^{2}},

as in standard QED. We also have that

(20)

\displaystyle 0=\mu\derivative{\mu}m_{b}=Z_{m}m\left(\frac{\mu}{m}\derivative{m}{\mu}+\frac{\mu}{Z_{m}}\derivative{Z_m}{\mu}\right),

so the anomalous dimension for
m
is

(21)

\displaystyle\gamma^{[1]}_{m}=-\frac{3e^{2}}{8\pi^{2}}\frac{1}{1-\kappa^{2}},

which reproduces the standard QED result upon taking the limit
\kappa\to 0
. Furthermore, we capture the running of
\kappa
in the quantity

(22)

\displaystyle\gamma^{[1]}_{\kappa}=\frac{e^{2}}{12\pi^{2}}.

Finally, for the gauge fixing parameter, we find

(23)

\displaystyle\gamma_{\xi_{1}^{-1}}^{[1]}=\frac{e^{2}}{6\pi^{2}}.

The remaining parameters in our Lagrangian do not run at one loop in this basis. The picture changes once we perform a change of basis in field space to remove the mixing term
-\frac{\kappa_{b}}{2}F_{1\,b}^{\mu\nu}F_{2\,b,\mu\nu}
in the Lagrangian, which will be the subject of the next section.

3.
Basis Without Kinetic Mixing

We can rotate in field space to trade the mixing term in the Lagrangian (
1
) for an explicit dark-photon-fermion coupling and non-diagonal gauge fixing functions. To do so, we start from our original Lagrangian and substitute

(24)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}A_{1\,b}^{\mu}\\[2.0pt]
A_{2\,b}^{\mu}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1&-\frac{\kappa_{b}}{\sqrt{1-\kappa_{b}^{2}}}\\
0&\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\kappa_{b}^{2}}}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{A}_{1\,b}^{\mu}\\[2.0pt]
\widetilde{A}_{2\,b}^{\mu}\end{pmatrix}

to remove the kinetic mixing.
Note that the choice of rotation matrix is not unique. One can always apply an additional orthogonal rotation keeping the kinetic terms canonically normalized, but we do not need this extra degree of freedom here.
Dropping the tilde on the field variables, the original Lagrangian becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}(F_{1\,b}^{\mu\nu})^{2}-\frac{1}{4}(F_{2\,b}^{\mu\nu})^{2}+i\overline{\psi}_{b}\gamma_{\mu}\partial^{\mu}\psi_{b}-\widetilde{m}_{b}\overline{\psi}_{b}\psi_{b}

(25)

\displaystyle-e_{1\,b}\overline{\psi}_{b}\gamma_{\mu}A_{1\,b}^{\mu}\psi_{b}-e_{2\,b}\overline{\psi}_{b}\gamma_{\mu}A_{2\,b}^{\mu}\psi_{b}-\frac{1}{2\widetilde{\xi}_{1\,b}}\left(\partial_{\mu}A_{1\,b}^{\mu}+\frac{e_{2\,b}}{e_{1\,b}}\partial_{\mu}A_{2\,b}^{\mu}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2\widetilde{\xi}_{2\,b}}(\partial_{\mu}\widetilde{A}_{2\,b}^{\mu})^{2}+\frac{\widetilde{M}_{b}^{2}}{2}(A_{2\,b}^{\mu})^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{\mu}\phi_{b})^{2}-\widetilde{\xi}_{2\,b}\frac{\widetilde{M}_{b}^{2}}{2}\phi_{b}^{2},

with the new parameters satisfying

(26)

\displaystyle\widetilde{m}_{b}

\displaystyle=m_{b},

\displaystyle\qquad\widetilde{M}_{b}

\displaystyle=\frac{M_{b}}{\sqrt{1-\kappa_{b}^{2}}},

\displaystyle\qquad e_{1\,b}

\displaystyle=e_{b},

\displaystyle e_{2\,b}

\displaystyle=-\frac{e_{b}\kappa_{b}}{\sqrt{1-\kappa_{b}^{2}}},

\displaystyle\qquad\widetilde{\xi}_{1\,b}

\displaystyle=\xi_{1\,b},

\displaystyle\qquad\widetilde{\xi}_{2\,b}

\displaystyle=(1-\kappa_{b}^{2})\,\xi_{2\,b}.

Now we have an explicit electron-dark-photon coupling, but the kinetic mixing is removed. The rotation also produced non-diagonal gauge fixing functions, but we will show explicitly how the off-diagonal contributions cancel when computing any correlation function in the next section.

3.1.
Tree-Level Propagator

Due to the non-diagonal gauge fixing function, the matrix-valued gauge boson propagator still has non-zero off-diagonal elements.
The Fourier transformed quadratic terms are

(27)

P^{-1}:=\begin{pmatrix}-p^{2}g_{\mu\nu}+\left(1-\frac{1}{\xi_{1\,b}}\right)p_{\mu}p_{\nu}&-\frac{e_{2\,b}}{e_{1\,b}}\frac{1}{\xi_{1\,b}}p_{\mu}p_{\nu}\\
-\frac{e_{2\,b}}{e_{1\,b}}\frac{1}{\xi_{1\,b}}p_{\mu}p_{\nu}&(-p^{2}+M_{b}^{2})g_{\mu\nu}+\left(1+\frac{e_{2\,b}}{e_{1\,b}}\frac{1}{\xi_{1\,b}}-\frac{1}{\xi_{2\,b}}\right)p_{\mu}p_{\nu}\end{pmatrix},

and thus we find for the propagator

(28)

\displaystyle iP^{\mu\nu}=\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{i}{p^{2}}\left(g^{\mu\nu}-\left(\frac{(1-\xi_{1\,b})}{p^{2}}-\frac{e_{2\,b}}{e_{1\,b}}C(p^{2})\right)p^{\mu}p^{\nu}\right)&iC(p^{2})\frac{p^{\mu}p^{\nu}}{p^{2}}\\
iC(p^{2})\frac{p^{\mu}p^{\nu}}{p^{2}}&-\frac{i}{\left(p^{2}-M_{b}^{2}\right)}\left(g^{\mu\nu}-\frac{(1-\xi_{2\,b})p^{\mu}p^{\nu}}{\left(p^{2}-M_{b}^{2}\xi_{2\,b}\right)}\right)\end{pmatrix},

where

(29)

\displaystyle C(p^{2})=\xi_{2\,b}\frac{e_{2\,b}}{e_{1\,b}}\frac{1}{p^{2}-M_{b}^{2}\xi_{2\,b}}.

We see that, even having removed the
\kappa
-term in the Lagrangian, the off-diagonal elements are still non-vanishing. However, they are unphysical, as indicated by the overall factor of
\xi_{2\,b}
. To see this explicitly in a general gauge, let us define

(30)

\displaystyle iP^{\mu\nu}_{11}:=i\widetilde{P}^{\mu\nu}_{11}-i\frac{e_{2\,b}}{e_{1\,b}}C(p^{2})\frac{p^{\mu}p^{\nu}}{p^{2}},

i.e., split
P^{\mu\nu}_{11}
into the usual photon propagator
\widetilde{P}^{\mu\nu}_{11}
plus an additional piece.

Now, let us consider an arbitrary Feynman diagram in this theory. For every gauge boson propagator that appears in the diagram, one also has to consider the diagrams obtained by replacing that propagator with either a mixed propagator or the one of the other gauge boson. Summing over these three contributions, taking into account the different couplings of attaching each of these propagators to the rest of the diagram, gives

(31)

\displaystyle e_{1\,b}^{2}P^{\mu\nu}_{11}+e_{1\,b}e_{2\,b}P^{\mu\nu}_{12}+e_{2\,b}^{2}P^{\mu\nu}_{22}=e_{1\,b}^{2}\widetilde{P}^{\mu\nu}_{11}+e_{2\,b}^{2}P^{\mu\nu}_{22}.

This shows that the off-diagonal part of the gauge-fixing term has no effect and the theory behaves as if we had a massless photon and a massive vector boson without kinetic mixing, i.e., with diagonal tree-level propagators.

3.2.
UV Poles

In this basis without the mixed kinetic term, loop corrections still induce a non-vanishing off-diagonal gauge boson two-point function, even though there is no tree-level term in the Lagrangian. Without the parameter
\kappa
, we cannot make the individual off-diagonal two-point functions finite. Instead, as renormalization conditions we impose all observables, i.e. the masses and
S
-matrix elements, to be finite (cf.
[
1
]
). The two-point functions are not physical observables, and thus, they are not required to be finite. Accordingly, we introduce renormalization constants only for the two masses and two couplings of the theory (in the same fashion as in
[
1
]
for the scalar case):

(32)

\displaystyle\widetilde{m}_{b}=Z_{\widetilde{m}}\widetilde{m},\qquad e_{1\,b}=Z_{e_{1}}e_{1},\qquad\widetilde{M}_{b}=\sqrt{Z_{\widetilde{M}}}\widetilde{M},\qquad e_{2\,b}=Z_{e_{2}}e_{2}.

Following
[
1
]
, we do not introduce field rescalings here but, equivalently, we will instead include the residues of the two-point functions explicitly in the LSZ theorem in
section

3.3
.
As before, we decompose the renormalization factors as
Z_{i}=1+\delta_{i}
, and the counterterm Lagrangian then reads

(33)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\delta}=

\displaystyle-e_{1}\delta_{e_{1}}\gamma_{\mu}\overline{\psi}A_{1}^{\mu}\psi-e_{2}\delta_{e_{2}}\gamma_{\mu}\overline{\psi}A_{2}^{\mu}\psi-\widetilde{m}\delta_{\widetilde{m}}\overline{\psi}\psi+\delta_{\widetilde{M}}\frac{\widetilde{M}^{2}}{2}(A_{2}^{\mu})^{2}-\xi_{2}\frac{\widetilde{M}^{2}}{2}\delta_{\widetilde{M}}\phi^{2}.

The Feynman rules for the counterterms are

(34)

Many of these objects only differ by constants originating from the vertex factors. We find the UV poles

(36)

\begin{split}i\Pi_{11}^{[1]\,\mu\nu}&=\frac{-ie_{1}^{2}}{12\pi^{2}}(p^{2}g^{\mu\nu}-p^{\mu}p^{\nu})\frac{1}{\epsilon},\qquad i\Pi_{12}^{[1]\,\mu\nu}=-\frac{ie_{1}e_{2}}{12\pi^{2}}(p^{2}g^{\mu\nu}-p^{\mu}p^{\nu})\frac{1}{\epsilon},\\
i\Pi_{22}^{[1]\,\mu\nu}&=\frac{-ie_{2}^{2}}{12\pi^{2}}(p^{2}g^{\mu\nu}-p^{\mu}p^{\nu})\frac{1}{\epsilon}+i\delta_{\widetilde{M}}\widetilde{M}^{2}g^{\mu\nu},\\
i\Sigma^{[1]}&=\frac{i}{16\pi^{2}\epsilon}\left(\left(e_{1}^{2}\widetilde{\xi}_{1}+e_{2}^{2}\widetilde{\xi}_{2}\right)\not\!{p}-\left(e_{1}^{2}(\widetilde{\xi}_{1}+3)+e_{2}^{2}(\widetilde{\xi}_{2}+3)\right)\widetilde{m}\right)-i\widetilde{m}\delta_{\widetilde{m}},\\
i\Gamma_{1}^{[1]\,\mu}&=-\frac{ie_{1}}{16\pi^{2}\epsilon}\gamma^{\mu}(\widetilde{\xi}_{1}e_{1}^{2}+\widetilde{\xi}_{2}e_{2}^{2})-ie_{1}\delta_{e_{1}}\gamma^{\mu},\\
i\Gamma_{2}^{[1]\,\mu}&=-\frac{ie_{2}}{16\pi^{2}\epsilon}\gamma^{\mu}(\widetilde{\xi}_{1}e_{1}^{2}+\widetilde{\xi}_{2}e_{2}^{2})-ie_{2}\delta_{e_{2}}\gamma^{\mu}.\end{split}

3.3.
Resummed Green’s Functions, LSZ Theorem and Renormalization

The crucial observation is that in this basis, even if we were to renormalize all parameters, there is no counterterm able to absorb the divergence of
\Pi_{12}^{[1]\,\mu\nu}
, so the off-diagonal element of the full Green’s function,
G_{12}
, cannot be made finite. This is not a problem as it is only the poles of
G_{ij}
(i.e. the masses of the particles) that are observables and in need of renormalization. The other observables that have to be finite are the
S
-matrix elements calculated from the LSZ theorem, which differ from amputated, renormalized Green’s functions due to the mixing on the external legs. After accounting for this
[
3
]
, the
S
-matrix elements may receive a contribution from
G_{12}
. However, this divergence can be absorbed by the coupling counterterms.

The physical masses of the photon and dark photon are the poles of the matrix-valued two-point function
G^{\mu\nu}
. They may be found as the zeros of the determinant of the inverse of
G^{\mu\nu}

[
3
]
:

(37)

\det\left(G^{-1}\right)=\det\left((1+\Pi P)P^{-1}\right)=\det\left(P^{-1}+\Pi\right),

where we again suppressed the Lorentz indices. This allows us to determine the poles without explicitly resumming all 1PI contributions. The determinant evaluates to

(38)

\begin{split}\det(G^{-1})=\frac{(p^{2})^{4}}{\widetilde{\xi}_{1}\widetilde{\xi}_{2}}\left(p^{2}\Delta-\widetilde{M}^{2}(1+\Pi_{11})\right)^{3}\left(p^{2}-\widetilde{\xi}_{2}\widetilde{M}^{2}\right),\hskip 9.24994pt\Delta=\det\begin{pmatrix}\Pi_{11}+1&\Pi_{12}\\
\Pi_{12}&\Pi_{22}+1\end{pmatrix}.\end{split}

The two gauge-independent pole masses
\widetilde{m}_{\gamma,P}^{2}
and
\widetilde{M}_{P}^{2}
are, respectively, the solutions of
p^{2}=0
(i.e. the photon mass) and
\widetilde{M}^{2}(1+\Pi_{11})-p^{2}\Delta=0
, which determines the mass of the dark photon.

As our propagators have a non-standard structure, we also resum the 1PI diagrams to provide the explicit results for the resummed Green’s functions in general
R_{\xi}
-gauge. Note that also in this basis,
\Pi_{ij}^{\mu\nu}
is transversal due to gauge invariance. In standard QED, the resummed photon two-point function is

(39)

\displaystyle iG^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{-i}{p^{2}(1+\Pi)}\left(g^{\mu\nu}-\frac{(1-(1+\Pi)\xi)p^{\mu}p^{\nu}}{p^{2}}\right).

Once we add the dark photon, the expressions become slightly more involved and matrix-valued. In this basis, the components of the resummed Green’s function are

\displaystyle G_{11}^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{p^{2}}\left(\frac{p^{2}(1+\Pi_{22})-\widetilde{M}^{2}}{\Delta p^{2}-\widetilde{M}^{2}(1+\Pi_{11})}\left(g^{{\mu\nu}}-\frac{p^{\mu}p^{\nu}}{p^{2}}\right)+\left(\tilde{\xi}_{1}+\frac{e_{2}^{2}}{e_{1}^{2}}\frac{p^{2}\widetilde{\xi}_{2}}{p^{2}-\widetilde{\xi}_{2}\widetilde{M}^{2}}\right)\frac{p^{\mu}p^{\nu}}{p^{2}}\right),

(40)

\displaystyle G_{12}^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Pi_{12}}{\Delta p^{2}-\widetilde{M}^{2}(1+\Pi_{11})}\left(g^{\mu\nu}-\frac{p^{\mu}p^{\nu}}{p^{2}}\right)+\frac{e_{2}}{e_{1}}\frac{\widetilde{\xi}_{2}}{p^{2}-\widetilde{\xi}_{2}\widetilde{M}^{2}}\frac{p^{\mu}p^{\nu}}{p^{2}},

\displaystyle G_{22}^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{\Delta p^{2}-\widetilde{M}^{2}(1+\Pi_{11})}\left((1+\Pi_{11})\left(g^{\mu\nu}-\frac{p^{\mu}p^{\nu}}{p^{2}-\widetilde{\xi}_{2}\widetilde{M}^{2}}\right)+\widetilde{\xi}_{2}\Delta\frac{p^{\mu}p^{\nu}}{p^{2}-\widetilde{\xi}_{2}\widetilde{M}^{2}}\right).

Note that the poles coincide with the zeros of
\det(G^{-1})=0
. Furthermore, note that, even though the kinetic mixing is removed in this basis, these results are modified due to the presence of the off-diagonal gauge fixing terms in the tree-level propagator. However, just as at tree-level, there is a cancellation of the same type as in
eq.

31
also here, on the level of the resummed Green’s function.

For the pole mass
\widetilde{M}_{P}^{2}
up to one-loop order, we find

(41)

\displaystyle(\widetilde{M}_{P}^{2})^{[0]}+(\widetilde{M}_{P}^{2})^{[1]}=\widetilde{M}^{2}\left(1-\frac{e_{2}^{2}}{12\pi^{2}}\frac{1}{\epsilon}+\delta^{[1]}_{\widetilde{M}}\right),

and thus,

(42)

\displaystyle\delta^{[1]}_{\widetilde{M}}=\frac{e_{2}^{2}}{12\pi^{2}}\frac{1}{\epsilon}.

For the electron, the resummed two-point function is

(43)

\displaystyle G_{e}(\not\!{p})=\frac{1}{\not\!{p}-\widetilde{m}+\Sigma(\not\!{p})}=\frac{1}{\not\!{p}(1+\Sigma_{V})-\widetilde{m}(1-\Sigma_{S})},\qquad\Sigma(\not\!{p})=\not\!{p}\Sigma_{V}+\widetilde{m}\Sigma_{S}.

We require the physical pole mass to one-loop order,

(44)

\displaystyle\widetilde{m}^{[0]}_{P}+\widetilde{m}^{[1]}_{P}=\widetilde{m}(1+\delta^{[1]}_{\widetilde{m}}-\Sigma^{[1]}_{S}-\Sigma^{[1]}_{V}),

to be finite, which fixes the counterterm

(45)

\displaystyle\delta^{[1]}_{\widetilde{m}}=\Sigma^{[1]}_{V}+\Sigma^{[1]}_{S}=-\frac{3}{16\pi^{2}\epsilon}(e_{1}^{2}+e_{2}^{2}).

At this pole we define the residue

(46)

\displaystyle Z_{e}=\left(\lim\limits_{\not\!{p}\to\widetilde{m}_{P}}(\not\!{p}-\widetilde{m}_{P})G_{e}(\not\!{p})\right)=\left.\frac{1}{1+\Sigma_{V}}\right|_{\,\not\!{p}=\widetilde{m}_{P}}.

The Källén-Lehmann representation for vector bosons in covariant gauge may be written as
[
17
]

(47)

-G_{ij}(p^{2})=Z_{ij}^{(k)}\left(\frac{g^{\mu\nu}-p^{\mu}p^{\nu}/M_{k}^{2}}{p^{2}-M_{k}^{2}+i\varepsilon}+z_{ij}^{(k)}\frac{p^{\mu}p^{\nu}/M_{k}^{2}}{p^{2}-\xi M_{k}^{2}+i\varepsilon}\right)+\int_{M_{0}^{2}}^{\infty}\rho_{ij}(s)\frac{g^{\mu\nu}-p^{\mu}p^{\nu}/s}{p^{2}-s+i\varepsilon}ds,

where
Z_{ij}^{(k)}
and
z_{ij}^{(k)}
are scalar numbers and
\rho_{ij}(s)
a positive measure. The factor
Z_{ij}^{(k)}
is simply the residue at the pole of the
ij
-two-point function at the state with mass
M_{k}^{2}
. For practical purposes it is easiest to find the residue by fixing the gauge to Feynman gauge,
\widetilde{\xi}_{1}\to 0
,
\widetilde{\xi}_{2}\to 0
. In particular, we find

(48)

\displaystyle Z_{11}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{p^{2}\to 0}p^{2}\left(\frac{1}{p^{2}}\frac{(p^{2}(1+\Pi_{22})-\widetilde{M}^{2})}{\Delta p^{2}-\widetilde{M}^{2}(1+\Pi_{11})}\right)=\left.\frac{1}{1+\Pi_{11}}\right|_{p^{2}=0},

\displaystyle Z_{22}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{p^{2}\to\widetilde{M}_{P}^{2}}(p^{2}-\widetilde{M}_{P}^{2})\frac{(1+\Pi_{11})}{\Delta p^{2}-\widetilde{M}^{2}(1+\Pi_{11})}=\left.\frac{1+\Pi_{11}}{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}p^{2}}(\Delta p^{2}-\widetilde{M}^{2}(1+\Pi_{11}))}\right|_{p^{2}=\widetilde{M}_{P}^{2}},

\displaystyle Z_{12}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\lim_{p^{2}\to 0}p^{2}\left(\frac{-\Pi_{12}}{\Delta p^{2}-\widetilde{M}^{2}(1+\Pi_{11})}\right)=0,

\displaystyle Z_{12}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\lim_{p^{2}\to\widetilde{M}_{P}^{2}}(p^{2}-\widetilde{M}_{P}^{2})\left(\frac{-\Pi_{12}}{\Delta p^{2}-\widetilde{M}^{2}(1+\Pi_{11})}\right)=\left.\frac{-\Pi_{12}}{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}p^{2}}(\Delta p^{2}-\widetilde{M}^{2}(1+\Pi_{11}))}\right|_{p^{2}=\widetilde{M}_{P}^{2}}.

We denote the two physical three-point amplitudes for
e\bar{e}\gamma
and
e\bar{e}\gamma_{\text{dark}}
by
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
, respectively. They may be computed from the LSZ theorem after accounting for mixing on the external legs, as was demonstrated for a scalar theory in
[
3
]
. In our case, the
S
-matrix elements are then given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{i}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\left(\lim\limits_{\not\!{p}\to\widetilde{m}_{P}}(\not\!{p}-\widetilde{m}_{P})G_{e}(\not\!{p})\right)^{2}\lim\limits_{p^{2}\to\widetilde{m}_{i}^{2}}(p^{2}-\widetilde{m}_{i}^{2})G_{ij}^{\mu\nu}(p^{2})\frac{1}{\sqrt{Z_{e}^{2}Z_{ii}^{(i)}}}\Gamma_{j,\nu}

(49)

\displaystyle=\left(\lim\limits_{\not\!{p}\to\widetilde{m}_{P}}(\not\!{p}-\widetilde{m}_{P})G_{e}(\not\!{p})\right)\lim\limits_{p^{2}\to\widetilde{m}_{i}^{2}}(p^{2}-\widetilde{m}_{i}^{2})G_{ii}^{\mu\nu}(p^{2})\frac{1}{\sqrt{Z_{ii}^{(i)}}}\Gamma_{i,\nu}

\displaystyle+\left(\lim\limits_{\not\!{p}\to\widetilde{m}_{P}}(\not\!{p}-\widetilde{m}_{P})G_{e}(\not\!{p})\right)\left(\lim\limits_{p^{2}\to\widetilde{m}_{i}^{2}}(p^{2}-\widetilde{m}_{i}^{2})G_{ij}^{\mu\nu}(p^{2})\frac{1}{\sqrt{Z_{ii}^{(i)}}}\Gamma_{j,\nu}\right)\Bigg|_{i\neq j},

with an implicit sum over the repeated index
j
. Here, we separated the contribution from attaching diagonal or off-diagonal gauge boson legs.
The divergent piece of the amplitude with an external photon at one loop is given by

(50)

\mathcal{A}_{1}^{[1]\,\mu}\sim-e_{1}\gamma^{\mu}\left(\delta_{e_{1}}^{[1]}-\frac{e_{1}^{2}}{24\pi^{2}}\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right)\implies\delta^{[1]}_{e_{1}}=\frac{e_{1}^{2}}{24\pi^{2}}\frac{1}{\epsilon}.

Similarly, for the dark photon amplitude, we find

(51)

\mathcal{A}_{2}^{[1]\,\mu}\sim-e_{2}\gamma^{\mu}\left(\delta_{e_{2}}^{[1]}-\frac{e_{2}^{2}}{24\pi^{2}}\frac{1}{\epsilon}-\frac{e_{1}^{2}}{12\pi^{2}}\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right)\implies\delta^{[1]}_{e_{2}}=\frac{e_{1}^{2}}{12\pi^{2}\epsilon}+\frac{e_{2}^{2}}{24\pi^{2}\epsilon}.

Note that the extra contribution in
\delta^{[1]}_{e_{2}}
proportional to
e_{1}^{2}
comes from the fact that we also need to absorb the divergence originating from
G_{12}
.
Next, we study the RGEs for all physical parameters in this basis and then compare them to the results in the basis with kinetic mixing.

3.4.
Renormalization Group

Again, we give the RGEs here to one-loop order. For
e_{1}
, we find the same as in standard QED,

(52)

\displaystyle\beta^{[1]}(e_{1})=\frac{e_{1}^{3}}{12\pi^{2}}.

However, for
e_{2}
we have

(53)

\displaystyle\beta^{[1]}(e_{2})=-e_{2}\left(\epsilon+\frac{\mu}{Z_{e_{2}}}\left(\derivative{Z_{e_2}}{e_1}\derivative{e_1}{\mu}+\derivative{Z_{e_2}}{e_2}\derivative{e_2}{\mu}\right)\right)=\frac{e_{1}^{2}e_{2}}{6\pi^{2}}+\frac{e_{2}^{3}}{12\pi^{2}}.

The anomalous dimension for
\widetilde{m}
is

(54)

\displaystyle\gamma_{\widetilde{m}}^{[1]}=-\frac{\mu}{Z_{\widetilde{m}}}\derivative{Z_{\widetilde m}}{\mu}=-\frac{\mu}{Z_{\widetilde{m}}}\left(\derivative{Z_{\widetilde m}}{e_1}\derivative{e_1}{\mu}+\derivative{Z_{\widetilde m}}{e_2}\derivative{e_2}{\mu}\right)=-\frac{3}{8\pi^{2}}(e_{1}^{2}+e_{2}^{2}).

In this basis, we also have an anomalous dimension for the dark photon mass
\widetilde{M}
,

(55)

\displaystyle\gamma_{\widetilde{M}}^{[1]}=-\frac{\mu}{\sqrt{Z_{\widetilde{M}}}}\derivative{\sqrt{Z_{\widetilde M}}}{\mu}=-\frac{1}{2}\derivative{\delta_{\widetilde M}}{e_2}\mu\derivative{e_2}{\mu}=\frac{e_{2}^{2}}{12\pi^{2}}.

4.
Comparison of the Two Bases

For the convenience of the reader, we give again the full set of all non-vanishing RGEs in the two bases. In the basis with kinetic mixing, we found

(56)

\displaystyle\beta^{[1]}(e)=\frac{e^{3}}{12\pi^{2}},\qquad\gamma_{m}^{[1]}=-\frac{3e^{2}}{8\pi^{2}}\frac{1}{1-\kappa^{2}},\qquad\gamma_{\kappa}^{[1]}=\frac{e^{2}}{12\pi^{2}},\qquad\gamma_{\xi_{1}^{-1}}^{[1]}=\frac{e^{2}}{6\pi^{2}}.

In the basis without kinetic mixing, we have

(57)

\beta^{[1]}(e_{1})=\frac{e_{1}^{3}}{12\pi^{2}},\quad\beta^{[1]}(e_{2})=\frac{e_{1}^{2}e_{2}}{6\pi^{2}}+\frac{e_{2}^{3}}{12\pi^{2}},\quad\gamma_{\widetilde{m}}^{[1]}=-\frac{3}{8\pi^{2}}(e_{1}^{2}+e_{2}^{2}),\quad\gamma_{\widetilde{M}}^{[1]}=\frac{e_{2}^{2}}{12\pi^{2}}.

The relations between the parameters in the two bases have the same form for the renormalized parameters as for the bare ones. Thus, according to
eq.

26
, we have

(58)

\begin{split}\widetilde{m}&=m,\quad\widetilde{M}=\frac{M}{\sqrt{1-\kappa^{2}}},\quad e_{1}=e,\quad e_{2}=-\frac{e\kappa}{\sqrt{1-\kappa^{2}}}.\end{split}

Here we give only the relations for the physical parameters, since we will be interested in matching their RGEs only.

The matching of
e
with
e_{1}
and of
m
with
\widetilde{m}
is trivial: Expressing
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
on the right-hand side of the RGEs in terms of
e
and
\kappa
, we immediately find that

(59)

\displaystyle\beta^{[1]}(e)=\beta^{[1]}(e_{1}),\qquad\gamma_{m}^{[1]}=\gamma_{\widetilde{m}}^{[1]}.

To match the remaining physical parameters, we first differentiate the relations of the parameters in the two bases,

(60)

\displaystyle\gamma_{\widetilde{M}}^{[1]}=\frac{\mu}{\widetilde{M}}\derivative{\mu}\widetilde{M}=\sqrt{1-\kappa^{2}}\frac{\mu}{M}\derivative{\mu}\left(\frac{M}{\sqrt{1-\kappa^{2}}}\right)=\gamma_{M}^{[1]}+\sqrt{1-\kappa^{2}}\mu\derivative{\mu}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\kappa^{2}}},

and

(61)

\displaystyle\beta^{[1]}(e_{2})=\mu\derivative{\mu}e_{2}=\mu\derivative{\mu}\left(-\frac{e\kappa}{\sqrt{1-\kappa^{2}}}\right)=-\frac{\kappa}{\sqrt{1-\kappa^{2}}}\beta^{[1]}(e)-e\mu\derivative{\mu}\frac{\kappa}{\sqrt{1-\kappa^{2}}}.

From
eq.

60
we have

(62)

\displaystyle\sqrt{1-\kappa^{2}}\mu\derivative{\mu}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\kappa^{2}}}=\gamma_{\widetilde{M}}^{[1]}-\gamma_{M}^{[1]},

and with the chain rule, we find

(63)

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa}{1-\kappa^{2}}\mu\derivative{\kappa}{\mu}=\gamma_{\widetilde{M}}^{[1]}-\gamma_{M}^{[1]}=\frac{e_{2}^{2}}{12\pi^{2}}\implies\gamma_{\kappa}=\frac{e^{2}}{12\pi^{2}}.

In the same fashion, from
eq.

61
we get

(64)

\displaystyle\mu\derivative{\mu}\frac{\kappa}{\sqrt{1-\kappa^{2}}}=-\frac{1}{e}\left(\frac{\kappa}{\sqrt{1-\kappa^{2}}}\beta(e)+\beta(e_{2})\right),

and thus

(65)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1-\kappa^{2})^{3/2}}\mu\derivative{\kappa}{\mu}=-\frac{1}{e}\left(\frac{\kappa}{\sqrt{1-\kappa^{2}}}\frac{e^{3}}{12\pi^{2}}+\frac{e_{1}^{2}e_{2}}{6\pi^{2}}+\frac{e_{2}^{3}}{12\pi^{2}}\right)\implies\gamma_{\kappa}=\frac{e^{2}}{12\pi^{2}}.

This shows that the RGEs of all physical parameters in the two bases can be matched consistently with a rotation matrix that runs itself.

5.
Conclusions

We have shown at one-loop order that in a theory with two
U(1)
gauge bosons, one can choose a renormalization scheme without kinetic mixing. This means that after the kinetic mixing has been rotated away, it is not necessary to reintroduce this parameter to absorb divergences of off-diagonal two-point functions. We showed this by explicitly matching two renormalization schemes for two different bases in field space: one where an additional
U(1)
gauge boson (dark photon) was added to QED with kinetic mixing and one where this mixing was rotated away. The calculations are done in general covariant
R_{\xi}
-gauge, and naively, the gauge fixing function obstructs diagonalizability of the tree-level propagator. However, this obstruction is explicitly gauge-dependent, and we have shown that it drops out at each loop order once all contributing diagrams are summed.

Importantly, in the second scheme, we only renormalize the physical observables, i.e. the masses and scattering amplitudes, and we show that the two schemes can be matched using scale-dependent field redefinitions.

For future work, we plan to extend this analysis to two loops and beyond. At this level, the physical principles described here imply non-trivial cancellations between sub-diagrams to ensure renormalizability. Elucidating this cancellation and proving it to all loop orders would be very illuminating.
